ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u14	Mr. Chairman. Thank you. This has to do with the fact that you will receive a letter from me during this debate. I thought it would be nice to inform you about that in advance. Many dairy farmers are in uncertainty about their future, but unfortunately there are also many poultry farmers in the Netherlands who are currently experiencing uncertain times. The highly pathogenic H5N8 avian influenza virus was found in several European countries in November in wild water birds and poultry holdings. The Netherlands has not been spared either. I have previously mentioned wild infected birds at a hobby farmer in Den Oever and an animal park in Rotterdam. Last Saturday, I informed you by letter that, unfortunately, highly pathogenic avian influenza was also detected at a duck farm in Biddinghuizen, in the Flevopolder. In addition to all the sites of the infected farms, a farm in the 1 kilometre area of Biddinghuizen has been cleared. Unfortunately, today I have to tell you that highly pathogenic avian influenza has again been detected in two duck farms in the same area. Both farms are located in the 3 km area around the outbreak found last Saturday. Once again, therefore, in accordance with European rules, we are re-establishing the 3 and 10 km zones. They largely overlap the areas set up on Saturday. Let me say that both the poultry sector and myself find these developments very worrying. The risks of further dissemination remain high. Tonight I will introduce a number of new measures to prevent the spread and introduction of ducks and turkey farms. Firstly, measures to reduce the risks of straw storage on the yard of duck farms and the transport of this straw to the stable, secondly, measures to allow the transport of ducks or turkeys to the slaughterhouse only after a clinical inspection and thirdly, measures to ensure that transport to or from holdings with ducks and turkeys is carried out directly and subject to compliance with the hygiene protocol. These measures are all designed to prevent further spread of the virus as far as possible. It is also important to ensure that the poultry sector is vigilant and that the measures imposed are properly implemented. The NVWA supervises this. So you will receive a letter about this in the course of this evening. I thought it would be appropriate to give you an oral statement on this matter. We are talking about the same spokesmen who are sitting here in the Chamber.

It was not for nothing that the European Commission said to the Netherlands in 2002: okay, you can drive more manure out on your farmland than is actually justified, so this derogation is more than the farmers in other countries are allowed, but we do attach an important condition to that. The total manure production should not exceed its present level. A system of animal rights was intended to prevent the pig and chicken population from growing further, and for cows there was a European production ceiling: milk quotas. But instead of embracing this restriction of production, the farmers' representatives have always opposed it. More and more had to be produced. The Netherlands has therefore always actively advocated the abolition of milk quotas in Brussels. CDA, VVD and PVV found these production restrictions, which not only had to control the disastrous environmental effects of the livestock, but also kept the prices of milk and meat high, but a thorn in the eye. There has been hard lobbying and success. As of 2015, the European milk quota was abolished. In the run-up to its abolition, which was referred to by LTO, the dairy boys and their political equivalents as liberation day, the dairy herd started to grow well from 2012. So much so that the announcement by the Rutte I cabinet, with Secretary of State Bleeker, that animal production rights on pigs and chickens could not be lifted, could not continue. That seemed like an important warning to me. But the growth of the dairy herd was not restricted. Cows with golden horns, LTO promised the dairy farmers. Every Chinese schoolchild should drink half a litre of milk a day, encouraged by the Dutch model projects in China. It couldn't be helped, while the independent science and government advisory bodies, such as the Planning Office for the Environment, already gave strong warnings about the consequences of the unbridled growth of the dairy herd. In 2010, the PBL said that production limits will remain necessary. In 2011, Cap Gemini repeated that warning. But the cabinet, encouraged by the vast majority of the Chamber and excited by LTO, was hearing deaf and seeing blind. That every week more family farms fell down and more megasthals rose, that the number of cows that still see the meadow decreased dramatically during their lives, that a foreman of ZLTO admitted years ago that about 40% of the manure was illegally dumped, that nature in the Netherlands is suffocated by a stitching rod, that no longer improves water quality for years and that the goals of the Water Framework Directive will never be met in this most cattle-proof country in the world; they chose to ignore all that. The Netherlands had to produce more dairy at all costs. The production restriction of chicken and pig manure was also raised through the so-called POR scheme, which pushed CDA and VVD through it. Therefore, there is now no room to shift anything into the sectoral manure ceilings that have been agreed. Even if pig farmers would like to stop giving the cowboys more room, there is no room for manoeuvre at all because of the political grievances and the desire to win electoral victory. The Netherlands simply produces much and much and much too much manure. We see what this will cost: the life of 200,000 cows and if we are unlucky 400,000. It costs thousands of dairy farmers more money than they can earn when the derogation is lost. The farmers were right to stand in the hallway with their boots. It costs many organic dairy farmers their future. They were also on trial here. It costs taxpayers millions of euros to compensate the dairy farmers for the huge mistakes that have been made. In the debate on the responsible growth of dairy farming, I drew attention to the question of where the milk actually comes from. Disgrace on the whole: how dare I talk about small talk when dealing with the dairy law? However, it is important to remind everyone today what dairy farming is all about: the annual birth of a calf, which is taken directly from its mother and then, if it is a bull, no longer has any value for the dairy farmer. Kalfjes who have to reach slaughter weight at the lowest possible cost, never see the whey and get no milk and attention from their mother, but large amounts of antibiotics not to get too sick in their short lives, that they wear out in large barns. The too light dying calves that are not economically worth raising are burned and then worn out as green electricity to citizens who want to live sustainably. How wretched, chairman, how terribly gruesome. The calves, the reason why cows make milk, are the big blind spot in dairy farming. The fact that cow and calf welfare is not part of the debate on dairy farming is foreign to the world. I want to hear from the Secretary of State how he's gonna make sure this moral blind spot is solved. The fact that animals are not mentioned in the debate on dairy farming is not the only peculiarity in this debate. Today we are talking about phosphate rights and land-based dairy farming, two ominous concepts that are worth looking at before we can discuss the present legislative proposal and all the problems it raises. Phosphate rights are the first thing we discuss. This bill brings rights to life. There are pig rights and poultry rights in Dutch law, but these are unfortunately not rights for pigs, chickens and turkeys. It means that farmers are allowed to keep a certain quantity of pigs and chickens. This bill creates phosphate rights, i.e. rights for farmers, although of course they are not necessarily there either. It is not a right to get a good price for a product, so that they do not have to live below the minimum standard of living with the whole family or to be protected from unfair competition by trade deals such as TTIP and CETA, no, it is the right to pollute. There is no such right, and that is a good thing. Everyone must take care of our shared environment and resources. No one has the right to leave that and so to pollute. A pollution right does not exist and should therefore not exist in an economic system and should certainly not be freely negotiable, as the cabinet proposes. Now I come to landboundness and land-based dairy farms. What exactly are those? As far as the Party for Animals is concerned, these are farms with their own land around the stable, where the animals can feed on. So that's not a megastal somewhere in North Holland or anywhere else, that runs the manure on rented land in Drenthe on which lilies are grown, ghost soils. This has nothing to do with closed circles and sustainable agriculture. Animal welfare is far from complete. What the cabinet calls "ground-bound" is not ground-bound. In preparing for the import of phosphate rights, major political mistakes have been made, which are now making sure that next year there will be no legal restriction on any further growth of the dairy herd, errors that will cause the derogation of all dairy farmers to be lost. Let us be clear that the Animal Party sees that the derogation leads to unacceptable environmental pollution. So we are not in favour of a derogation. The way in which we are now going to lose the derogation is killing farmers. It's a cold cleanup, so only the megastalcowboys will be standing up soon. That is not the future of agriculture that we see before us or the future that farmers deserve. There must be a clear answer to the question of how these mistakes could have happened. There's ten of them. Error one: abolition of the milk quota without replacing a production-limiting element, while for years we have been warned that this would exceed the environmental limits. Does the Secretary of State see, afterwards, that it was a big mistake not to have a stick behind the door and only to rely on the promises made by the foremen of LTO and NZO, while the dairy population's growth figures have shown an image of exponential growth since 2012? Does he also recognise that he shares this responsibility with parties such as CDA, VVD and PVV, which have thwarted any production restrictions on livestock farming? Error two: how could the Secretary of State not foresee that the issuance of phosphate rights would not be classified as State aid? I really don't understand any of that. With his predecessor, Secretary of State Dijksma, we discussed various possibilities for reducing production. She insisted that she had examined all the possibilities from the grievance and that only phosphate rights would be a viable option. She rejected our proposal for the introduction of grazing livestock units, in which social conditions would be taken into account in many more ways. It wouldn't be feasible. Phosphate rights, that was the way. And it turns out that the government has not assessed State aid? How does the Secretary of State explain this state of affairs? In the explanatory memorandum to the letter of amendment, he himself pointed out that the NO rights constituted State aid. Would he have known in advance that these phosphate rights would simply constitute illegal state aid? How is that possible? He insisted on the marketability of phosphate rights, but that, of course, meant that the European Commission would not agree. Yet there was no plan-B, no safety net. Will the Secretary of State continue to insist that it was not necessary to have a plan B? Editor-in-chief Geert Hekker van de Boerderij wrote a sharp column about it, with a rake analysis. So things are exactly the other way around than everyone thought. The story was that phosphate rights would be needed to stay within the ceiling and now it appears that the Netherlands can only import duties after it is below the ceiling. I really do not understand why the Secretary of State still insists on the marketability of phosphate rights. There's no good argument for that. Is he too scared to sell the VVD no? In fact, if phosphate rights were not to become negotiable, they could be introduced for the coming year and this would significantly increase the chances of maintaining the derogation. I'd like an explanation. As Weir also said beautifully, who would be better off being negotiable? The brokers, the banks, the lawyers, the traders, but not the farmers, not the cows, not the environment and not the taxpayers, who will soon have to pay for buying out the big cowboys, if, of course, next time it becomes clear that manure production will have to go back even further than what the government is now taking into account. Error three: the readings. The predecessor of the Secretary of State sent a letter in 2013 which, in her view, would be considered a reference date for the introduction of production limits. The Council of State and the Land Attorney cut that line of reasoning, which now rewards the big growers who built just more mega stables after that announcement, and simply stands in their right, something that, in her opinion, would be totally impossible. How did that happen? How is it that apparently no legal check has been made on the legal sustainability of that announcement by Secretary of State Dijksma? This Secretary of State wants us to believe that with the legislative proposals we are discussing today and with the plan presented by the sector, there is a solution that will save livestock farming and also the derogation. The only reason he has to keep doing that today is that he will be done in March 2017. In the progress of this file, more errors are stacked. The mistakes about the present package. Mistake four: according to the European Commission, phosphate rights should only be introduced if there is an extra environmental interest in it, if there is more environmental gain than there is a legal obligation under European rules. The Secretary of State has still not implemented this. This also means that the present application of phosphate rights will not pass the European test and that means: no production limit, no derogation and a disaster for animals, the environment and farmers. Error five: competition. The Secretary of State reports on the present package that he will have to check with Brussels to see if it fits into the competition rules. Network Thoroughly draws its own conclusion on this: no, it does not fit, because the State Secretary creates a cartel and that is not allowed. I think it is very likely that the Consumer and Market Authority and the European Commission will come to that conclusion. In other Member States, too, there is a crisis in livestock farming. If, for example, France announced a similar arrangement to that made by this State Secretary, the State Secretary immediately complained to the European Commission about illegal interference with the free market. The parties in the Chamber would be mercilessly behind him until he took action and managed to get the European Commission to intervene against France. I'm pretty sure. So this part of the solution package presented by the Secretary of State is not sustainable either. Error six: the feed trail. That mysterious word is the answer to everything, if we can believe the Secretary of State. Less phosphate in feed: the solution for all your manure problems. Sounds good of course, if it isn't that this fantasy has been promoted for about twenty years and hasn't delivered anything in the meantime. In 1997 it was already called: the railroad is largely exploited. That's how Willem Weil wrote last week in Farmhouse. The Secretary of State knows this, the sector knows that, and more importantly, Brussels knows it. After all, the transport track has been part of the nitrate action plans for many years, without any results. If the Secretary of State wishes to continue to argue in this debate today that the railroad is really the panacea, I challenge him to come up with concrete figures, in which he outlines the results of recent years and provides a substantiated forecast of the profits that can be achieved in 2017 in concrete terms. He can't, so this land is also falling away. Error seven: our meadows disappear. The cows disappear behind the closed doors of megastables. Grassland's being ploughed. There is another problem. I haven't heard anyone else talk about this, but it's going to be a mess. When the European common agricultural policy was last amended, one greening requirement was imposed on dairy farming as a condition for billions of income support. One requirement is that the area of permanent grassland should not decrease by more than 5%. Can the Secretary of State tell us where we are at the moment? It seems that we are already at that critical border. If more pasture is ploughed away, dairy farming loses its right to income support. And that is the end of this sector. I'd like to hear an extensive response. How is the Secretary of State going to ensure that pastures are preserved and grazed? His plans so far do not provide any incentive in this direction. Wrong eight: the problem control. Attorneys tell us that the current problem regulation is far from satisfactory and that the farmers affected by the current bill have a very great chance of winning the proceedings against the State, which means that the State will have to pay millions in compensation. Is that responsible budgetary policy? Has there even been a calculation made about what that will cost? With a view to a future parliamentary inquiry, I certainly do not rule out the possibility of it being carried out, and I would be grateful if the Secretary of State would give an honest answer. A poll also shows that a quarter of all dairy farmers really do not feel the need to set aside their own growth plans for maintaining the derogation in the interests of all farmers. The chance that this Secretary of State will succeed under much and much more difficult circumstances is not very great. Experts say 20%. I call that a very minor success factor. The biggest mistake of the package proposed by the cabinet and the dairy industry is that it is destroying the only dairy farming sector that is still doing a little, or even a lot, to produce sustainably: land-based extensive and organic farmers. The Secretary of State is focusing on a shrinkage of the dairy herd. According to Rabobank, 10% of dairy farmers will quit. That should give air to the rest of the sector. But can the Secretary of State confirm that especially the smaller, more extensive and ground-based family businesses will cease to exist in the coming years and that the space that will be released with this will be resurrected by the bigger boys? These are the dairy farmers who have filled their stables in recent years with cows who never get to see a pasture. Does he think that's desirable? Is the Secretary of State prepared to admit that his policy is killing the extensive livestock industry in our country and driving further industrialisation? I'd like some figures from the Secretary of State. Who's stopping? Who's going through? What does that do with the average number of cows, with groundboundness and grazing? For organic farmers, the bill is simply incredibly unfair. It is indigestible that you have to turn in to maintain a derogation that you do not use yourself. How wretched is it that the demand for organic dairy is growing and is greater than the Dutch supply, but that organic dairy farming still has to shrink because the cabinet has allowed the megastal cowboys to grow unbridled? How wretched is it that the only sector that actually works on land closes the loops, has a high priority for animal welfare and is working without agricultural poison and fertilizer, now that the lack of sensible and forward-looking policies is being undermined? "I've only been allowed to use 170 kilos of nitrogen from animal manure per hectare, while others have always been allowed to use 250 kilos. But I'm gonna have to tie in anyway. Then why is he not prepared to spare the organic dairy farmers? They did not contribute to the production of the manure surplus. More importantly, because the organic guidelines are based on environmentally friendly production, these farmers do not systematically dump too much manure on their land. In other words, they do not make use of the derogation. Why do they have to pay for the late attempts by the Secretary of State to maintain the derogation and for the failing policies that preceded it? Of course, this sector also produces manure, but this manure is used to a large extent on its own land, to produce animal feed or is used by organic farmers. In addition, organic farmers still feed part of common manure because there is not enough organic manure available. Isn't it crazy to want to reduce organic milk and manure production in favour of cowboys? The goat and sheep industry in our country also produces manure, but according to the State Secretary this is so little that this sector does not have to participate in attempts to get within the national manure ceiling. Then why does organic dairy farming have to? The Animal Party recommends that organic dairy farming should not be included in achieving the manure production ceiling and that the derogation of the goat and sheep industry should be given to organic farmers. This will not only ensure that the organic sector is not sacrificed to the bio-industry, but also that the goat and sheep industry no longer gets away with the stashing of thousands of animals in megalomania, with all the major risks to public health. I'd like to get a reaction to this. The only thing the Secretary of State intends to do for the biological sector is to compensate all or part of these, like the other land-based establishments with latent placement space, for the generic skimming. That is not only very vague, it is far from enough. The mandatory land binding of organic livestock farms is, according to the European organic regulations, different from that defined in the present legislative proposal. Organic legislation means that organic manure must always be used on certified organic land, either on the dairy farmer's own land or on the ground of another certified organic farm, which will then have to enter into a cooperation which is once again monitored annually by Skal. This also means that the land-boundness as contained in the bill is in the way of organic dairy farmers. They also place manure on arable farms which in turn depend on it for their organic fruit, vegetables and cereals. Does the Secretary of State not see that it is strange that the definition of land-boundness as currently contained in the law does give room to megastal farmers who sell their land to lily growers, while the cows stand on stables for the year, but do not allow for the closed recycling system as laid down in biological legislation? Is he gonna stand behind this? Or is he prepared to adapt it so that the ground-based rules promote sustainable agriculture in our country and stop the intensification of agriculture? A number of organic dairy farms are therefore not land-based according to the definition in the cabinet's bill. According to the State Secretary's own figures, this is about 46 companies in 2015. There aren't many of them. The Party of Animals thinks we should come to the aid of these companies. Is the Secretary of State prepared to consider all organic farms as land-based, including those which have so far met their mandatory land-based requirements in the form of cooperation with another organic certified company? Organic dairy farmers, together with land-based farmers and environmental and animal welfare organisations, have drawn up a plan to prevent chilly clean-up in the most sustainable dairy farming sectors. They propose a maximum number of cows per hectare and link the phosphate rights to be issued to possess enough land to produce the animal feed and feed the animals. That plan aims to switch to a fully land-based dairy farming within five years. In concrete terms, after that five-year transitional period, farmers may keep up to 2.3 livestock units per hectare, including young cattle. In order to avoid increasing production per cow at the expense of animal welfare, it also sets a maximum of 16,750 kilograms of milk per hectare. It is only fair that the big growers, those who have caused the problems, pay for the consequences and that land-based farmers are spared. The Secretary of State must agree with that, right? So in the first year, 2017, a substantial shrinkage of more than 2.3 cows per hectare is realized. That can be regulated by law. The sector will not realize that itself. I would like to table this plan as an amendment, but it is complicated, because it requires different laws to be amended. It's wise to do it. I would like to present it in detail to the Chamber, with a comprehensive response from the Secretary of State. I would like him to undertake to cooperate in the elaboration of this in order to assess whether or not the Court agrees. The advantage of the plan would be that the manure surplus in the Netherlands would decrease significantly and that the derogation could be maintained. For now, the Party for Animals says so. As has already been said, derogations are not sacred to us. The organic sector shows that a healthy livestock farming can do just fine with 170 kilos of phosphate per hectare. That's a lot better for the environment and nature. Plan B, as the organisations call it applicable, it also happens to be the title of the Party for Animals' electoral programme, but that, by the way, helps to make the whole dairy farming truly land-bound. That's what it takes. It is also in line with the plan for grazing livestock units, a system proposed by former CDA Minister Braks in 1990. It's a lot like this plan. It not only guarantees the soil-boundness and closure of the nutrient loops, but it can also prevent production from being increased unnaturally per animal. For example, grazing livestock units not only contribute to reducing the manure surplus, but also to improving animal welfare and animal health. In short, the proposal spares farmers who are already working truly soil-based or even organically and forces the megastable cowboys to make their operations sustainable and animal-friendly. This system will make small, extensive dairy farming in the Netherlands future-proof and will ensure the well-being of both farmers, animals and the environment. So get rid of the phosphate rights. Also away with trading it. Just back to the base, the bottom. You can keep cows if you have enough land to feed and feed them. ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u226	Mr. Chairman. We are at a crossroads of roads. Can we allow dairy farming to develop into an intensive farming operation, in which the cows are kept stable permanently? Or does dairy farming retain its land-based character, with the cow in the pasture? Which way does this cabinet choose, does this Secretary of State choose? When we look at what the Rutte Cabinets have brought to dairy farming, we see that chaos is a trump card. The release of the milk quota led the farmers to produce like madmen, stirred up by the mantra that is more better and more profitable. I still do not understand why the farmers' representatives, such as LTO, have not warned and have not intervened. The farmers have a high level of organisation and LTO could have prevented much. The Dutch dairy farmers Trade Union provided it, but had to sell it, just like the Dutch Dairymen Board. The banks, the Rabobank first, driven by greed, failed. Many farmers were looking forward to the abolition of the milk quota system and the liberalisation of the milk market. Several advisers represented farmers for a long period of favourable milk prices. Let us note that the problems in dairy farming are now not only an unmanageable surplus of manure, but also disastrously low prices for dairy farmers, both due to overproduction. When the milk quota was abolished on 1 April 2015, the trees did not grow to the sky. For example, what the Dutch Dairymen Board had predicted happened. In a short time milk production continued to rise and the milk price collapsed like a plum pudding. This is also due to the disappointing demand from China and Russia's boycott, but it is mainly because there is much more milk produced. It's class one in high school in economics, isn't it? If the supply grows, the price drops. It's been foretold, not just by me. Especially Sieta of Keimpema warned against the abolition of the milk quota, as Cassandra did in ancient times for Troy's horse. Unfortunately, only the LTO-pleitbijders were listened to, who under the motto "bigger is better" helped the milk farmers of the rain in the drip. The cabinet of VVD and PvdA failed. When the decision to release the milk quota finally fell, it was already clear to many that the phosphate ceiling would be broken. The cabinet should have known about this. Now what? Now draconian measures are necessary. Now a large amount of regulation has been issued over dairy farmers in a short period of time. One law bumped over the other. Now there are legislative proposals which actually amount to quoting again, now by reducing the manure surplus. We must intervene, and the question is how. Where do we want to go? The SP wants the final result to be a ground-based livestock farming, with grazing, and to prevent the increase in scale. The family farm must remain the carrier of our dairy farming and must remain the carrier of the countryside. We must have long-term security for the future for our farmers. Another important point for us is that the cost of the solution is borne by the problem-makers; the most intensive dairy farms. Land-based farmers and organic farmers who are not to blame for exceeding the phosphate ceiling should be spared. If we look to the future, we must also send for it. What the cabinet has done so far has only led to economies of scale and less pasture. I may see the Secretary of State frowning, but the number of cows still in the meadow has fallen by 4% in one year. We may then discuss agreements and pastures with the sector, but the road to hell is paved with good intentions and covenants. Land-based farmers and organic farmers have the future. Let us therefore make sure that these farmers are treated in the same way and that the others are adjusted sharply, not the other way round. This legislation threatens to reward the intensive, bold growers and the farmers who do well, the land-based farms, have to pay the price for it. As far as the SP is concerned, there is a simple solution. We must move towards a system of non-negotiable rights, exempting all extensive farmers below 2.5 cows per hectare. Only cows over 2.5 hectares will need phosphate rights. That saves a lot of rules for extensive farmers. We have seen that the European Commission has rejected the plans because of the State aid frameworks. To be honest, I can also imagine. What does the Cabinet do? With the measures it proposes, it gives an estimated value of EUR 7 to 8 billion. It's going into the sector. Those are the rights. So you're making it difficult for newcomers. They'll have to pay later. What is the response of the Secretary of State to the fact that such a sum is donated as a right? If the government ever wants to reduce further in the future or to attach conditions to phosphate rights, for example through emission reduction or grazing, is that possible or should a huge amount of money suddenly be paid to buy back phosphate rights? Can we get a legal basis on that? What barriers does the Secretary of State create for future regulation? We would like to receive a letter on how that is legal before we vote on this law. The problems with the State aid frameworks are very easy to circumvent, namely by not making the rights negotiable. They do not then constitute a value and therefore do not constitute a State aid issue. Why doesn't the Secretary of State want to deal with this? Can the Secretary of State explain why he thinks that the new version of the law is due to the European test? What exactly has changed that it would be acceptable by now? Are the plans before us bringing our vision of the future closer? The law may not even be the most important in this, because the plans for 2017 will determine whether the derogation remains in place. It's very simple. If the 10% stopping farmers in 2017 are mainly land-based farmers or organic farmers, then we get further away from home. If the result is that land-based farmers will have to turn in ten cows in 2017 in order to get them back in 2018, you won't get anything out of it. Why is the plans for 2017 not in any way directed towards the desired future image of land-based and cow-in-the-wei? Is the Secretary of State prepared to realize that this is going to happen? We found that land-based farmers, such as the Network Fundamental, organic farmers and the Dutch Dairymen Board were not allowed to participate. At least I didn't see it. Why not? There is a chamber motion in which the Secretary of State is asked to allow land-based dairy farmers to be adequately represented in the Regiegroep phosphate and dairy farming. Then it's weird that they shouldn't talk prominently about the route for 2017, whereas the most important decisions are made there? How will they be involved in further planning in 2017 to get below the phosphate ceiling? What guarantees do we have that the proposed measures will be effective? The past is not very hopeful, because some of these measures are based on assumptions. This applies, for example, to the transport track, which Frits van der Schans of the research agency CLM said had had no effect in the past. I'd like a response from the Secretary of State on that point. What assurance is there that the targets will be met and how will these targets be measured in the interim? How can they be redirected? The present plans of LTO and the Secretary of State are still extremely vague and not worked out. How are we going to work towards a land-based dairy farming in 2017? What's that process gonna look like? How and when will the Chamber be involved? How does the Secretary of State intend to use the universally binding instrument? How does he intend to legitimize that there has not been a discussion with organic farmers about 2017? Not with the Dutch Dairymen Board, not with Network Solid. Does the Secretary of State intend to involve these groups in the preparation of the plans for 2017? There is also an approved chamber motion that the government is asking to allow land-based dairy farmers to be adequately represented in the control group phosphate and dairy farming. There is a nice plan of Network Solid, De Natuurweide, Bionext, Milieudefensie, the Nature and Environmental Federation and a number of dairy organisations as an alternative to the LTO plan. The aim of this plan is to achieve a fully land-based dairy farming within five years. A substantial contraction could also be achieved in 2017. This is very close to the SP proposal on the 2.5 cows per hectare. So we are very sympathetic to that. I would like the Secretary of State to look at these plans with an open mind and to ask whether that is a passable path and a possible alternative to the plans that exist on the part of the LTO. There are also lawyers who doubt whether the plans can be made universally binding for 2017. That clashes with competition rules and could indicate cartels. There are also farmers who say: we are going to challenge this general declaration. How does the Secretary of State see that? Whatever happens, it is extremely important that there should be a general statement. There must be compulsion behind it, otherwise the goals will not be realized. The legislative proposal now before us is not satisfactory, because land-based farmers and organic farmers are not being spared. Despite an adopted SP motion by my colleague, Mr Smaling, in which the Chamber states that land-based farmers must be protected, the proposal before us only partially respects them. This means that we are getting net away from the vision of the future that we are facing. We continue to stray from the path. The brutal intensive companies, which have filled their stables as quickly as possible, are rewarded and the extensive holders have to pay for their growth. Suppose an intensive farm with 150 cows added 100 after the milk quota was abolished. It had 250 on 2 July 2015 and now has to reduce 10%, approximately. Then he still has 225 cows, compared to the 150 he had before. On the other hand, an extensive farmer with 80 cows, who has just enough land to get rid of all his phosphate, is also reduced by 10%, to 72 cows. The growth flukes of the last few years therefore go under the line to favour intensive farmers. I'd like a response from the Secretary of State to that thought. In the present legislative proposal, land-based farmers are spared only in so far as they have sufficient latent space. The relief of discount is wrongly made dependent on the amount of latent space. We have tabled an amendment to ensure that land-based farmers are spared if they are cut. Is the Secretary of State prepared to accept that amendment? But the adoption of the amendment on land-based farmers has not solved all the problems. Another problem is that land-based farmers can no longer grow their farms because intensive livestock farming has become more intensive. Does the Secretary of State agree with the SP that this is wrong and that this will be seen as unfair among the land-based farmers? According to an estimate by the sector, 6,000 fine organic cows should be killed because of the present bill. Organic farmers must therefore reject some 6,000 cows, while they can dispose of all manure in a nature-friendly and responsible way. They shall not make use of the derogation. However, they are limited in their growth space and even have to shrink their businesses. "I have to bring cows to slaughter to make intensive livestock farming intensive." That tweeted an angry organic farmer. "You have to get on the phone because your neighbor's too fat" tweeted another. That discontent led to a signature action called "Save the Biocow." In a short time, 14,000 signatures have been collected. Even if the amendment is adopted in order to avoid land-based farmers, biofarmers will still be in trouble. These are the following groups. Roughly 10% were in the transition situation from regular to organic, i.e. from one type of farming to another, or to a better stable system. As a result, these farmers had a much lower number of cows at the reference date. 10% of organic farmers are non-ground-bound according to the phosphate definition, but according to the biodefinition. These farmers then have feed-fertilizer contracts with farmers nearby. A large part of it was working on expansion plans because the demand for organic dairy is growing. I have only talked about the farmers who are farmers at the moment. Future biofarmers will also run into barriers. Dairy farmers who want to switch to organic farming in the future will be in trouble. This does not apply to regular dairy farmers who want to switch, but to a pig farmer, for example, or to someone with a mixed farm who wants to switch. Current biofarmers who want to expand in the future have to buy phosphate rights, even if they have latent space. Let me remind you that the biological sector does not make use of the derogation and uses only 40% of the phosphate space they have based on their country. I would like to mention a number of examples of biofarmers who have been affected. For biofarmer Arjen Kool, 30 years old, this has great consequences. In Hei- en Boeicop, on the border of Zuid-Holland and Utrecht, he runs a organic dairy farm together with his parents and his uncle. Last year they built a new, larger stable that offers the animals more comfort. In the meantime, 95 dairy cows are on the wood shavings in the new clearing. The measures for Kool probably mean having 16 cows slaughtered, the number that has been added since last summer. That saves him €56,000 milk revenues per year. Or he has to pay 16 times €5,000, so €80,000 for new phosphate rights. But there are no phosphate rights for sale for the time being. It's also a question of whether he's getting that money together. Let me give you a second example. Max van Tilburg, a junior from Breukelen, is a young, starting bioboer. With his company he manages a cultural historical park landscape that was built in the seventeenth century. He gradually builds up his own livestock. He had no animals on 2 July 2015. He started on 14 July 2015 with 26 cows. He has 65 now and wants to go to 80 animals with young cattle. He has 61 hectares of land at his disposal. So he's got less than 1.7 cows per hectare. If there is no regulation for start-ups, he must have his 65 cows all slaughtered. And if there were phosphate rights for sale, he would have to buy rights for 80 cows. That would cost him 80 times €5,000 is €400,000. If he could borrow this huge amount, the corresponding interest rate will give an income reduction of almost €20,000 per year. Just a reminder: the average income of a dairy farmer is about €25,000 per year. Example three. Jan-Rein de Jong from Rotstergaast is a young bioboer. He is enthusiastic about nature management, where butterflies get his special attention. He has 100 hectares of land and was working to gradually grow his livestock from young cattle. Target was 100 cows on 100 hectares. On the date of 2 July 2015, 60 dairy cows had grown up. Jan-Rein must therefore miss 40 dairy cows. He'd already made a very big investment in land. The bank has now announced him to stand guard and wants to finance any additional phosphate rights, 40 times €5,000 is €200,000. If nothing happens, it means the end of his business. Example four. Beerd Flier from Oldebroek has a very good feed-fertilizer relationship with a farmer in the neighboring polder. At his company the first KoeLand-demostal was realized. In 2012 the company won a prize together with partners for this design, which was awarded by, yes, then Minister Gerda Verburg. The aim of KoeLand is a new type of livestock farming: low emissions, high animal welfare, low antibiotic use, energy neutral and fit into the landscape. Further development, development and final licensing and financing took more time than hoped. Eventually, the first cows could enter the new stable at the end of June 2015. The new stable was calculated at 114 cows. Due to lack of space during construction, the number of cows in the old stable was temporarily reduced by 13. That's why there were only 66. He's keeping at least 79 now, so we need to get 13 to the slaughter. An additional complication now appears to be his cooperation relationship with the organic farmer. In 2015 he was not ground-bound according to the phosphate definition, but according to biological legislation, in which a written cooperation with another biocompany is recognised as ground-bound. Think of it as a mixed business. If this cooperation does not count for his phosphate rights, he must slaughter another 10 cows. He can still rent the land of the farmer so that it comes into his name, but that doesn't help him because 2015 is the reference year. So at least 23 cows have to go to slaughter. That award-winning innovative stable will be half empty. If there are phosphate rights to buy, this costs 23 cows €115.000. If he wants to use the innovative stable normally, he has to buy a further 35 cows' fees for €175.000. Total cost: €290,000. Is that even possible at all? The four examples lead me to this question to the Secretary of State: what can he do for these people? Will the Secretary of State help these biofarmers? The intensification of dairy farming is also detrimental to pig farming, a sector which is already suffering for a long time from a lack of profitability. Farmers pay to be allowed to plant their manure on land. Now that the market is flooded by cow manure, pig farmers have to pay more to be allowed to sell manure. These are thousands and often tens of thousands of euros per company. The pig farmers already owe EUR 2.4 billion to Rabobank together. Can the Secretary of State quantify more precisely how much more the pig farmers are going to pay for the manure disposal through this fiasco? Does the Secretary of State agree that a possible conversion of pig rights to phosphate rights should not lead to higher greenhouse gas emissions? We have tabled an amendment to add greenhouse gases to the conditions for converting pig rights into phosphate rights and to add a veil to that. I would like to ask for the support of the Secretary of State for this amendment. I assume that it is not his intention that the conversion of pig rights to phosphate rights should lead to more greenhouse gas emissions. I would like to hear a commitment on this point. I'm coming to a conclusion. The CDA has submitted a proposal on the problem regime. We support that. I have outlined a number of problems, particularly in the bio-sector. There's been a lot of trouble and that's rubbing in one way or another. Apart from what you find, in individual companies a lot of things happen that you say: actually that's not good, actually it can't. The Secretary of State says: I have made a problem regulation that is too limited; I have to apply it because otherwise the system will fail. But it feels unfair. I therefore hope that we will find ways in which we can do something in a number of cases. I also hope that the committee of wise men will do so. We need to move towards a consistent policy. That is necessary. Not the delusion of the day should rule, but certainty for the long term. That's what farmers need. They need certainty, a long-term vision they can rely on. Security creates peace and perspective for businesses.

ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u231	Mr. Chairman. Following the abolition of the milk quota in 2015, dairy farming has grown in size. A property belonging to individual entrepreneurs. Growth is such that the associated phosphate production exceeds the rules that Brussels allows. Farmers are very concerned about the future of their business. I understand these concerns. I understand that people are awake from the question of whether they still have a viable business and whether they can still transfer their business to their son or daughter in the future; that is fierce. The Netherlands should not produce more than 172.9 million kilograms of phosphate from Brussels under the Nitrate Directive. This phosphate production ceiling was agreed more than ten years ago to reduce the environmental burden. At the time of the milk quota this was generally not a problem, but after its abolition the sector has grown in size and thus phosphate production. The phosphate production ceiling does not take into account significant developments in the sector. For example, most of the poultry manure is burned so that the manure does not enter the environment. Part of manure is exported in the form of grains of manure. Since 2012, the manure processing obligation has been in force and farmers have an obligation to process a significant part of the manure. In addition, there are many innovative developments. For example, the sector is able to turn manure into green fertilizer, which can reduce the use of chemical fertilizers. It's better for the environment. We are also able to extract phosphate from manure. Phosphate is an important raw material, for which the Netherlands is now dependent on imports from countries such as Morocco or Syria. These developments illustrate that the Netherlands does not have a manure problem, but a solution to challenges facing the sector. The fact that the Netherlands does not have a manure problem is also confirmed by the levels of nitrate in groundwater. Measurements by the derogation companies show that a large part of the water quality is in order. Green fertilizer and the removal of phosphates from residual products are important steps towards our environmental objectives and the circular economy. The Nitrates Directive also determines the amount of animal manure that farmers are allowed to drive on the arable land. The Netherlands has an exemption from this rule and is allowed to apply more manure; that is the so-called derogation. A condition for derogation is that the Netherlands produces within the phosphate production ceiling. This is where the shoe wrings. With Dutch dairy farming producing more phosphate than Brussels allows, the Netherlands runs the risk that the derogation will be withdrawn and, worse still, that a new derogation will not be negotiated, which should enter into force from 1 January 2018. Brussels is sticking to the agreements that were made a long time ago. These agreements are rigid and do not allow for innovation and for the Dutch solutions that have just been mentioned. We are facing a difficult task. We want to offer entrepreneurs perspective and space for business development and encourage farmers to start working on innovations. However, we are not given this space, because if dairy farming does not produce below the phosphate ceiling in 2017, there is a good chance that the current derogation will be withdrawn or, worse still, that negotiations on a new derogation will not be started.

ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u362	Mr. Chairman. It's a special time. As you know, before this debate, I came back earlier from a trip to California with agricultural companies, food and technology companies. For me, it's still somewhere at the end of the afternoon, so I can take it for a while. Unfortunately, we started with a full gallery, the buses had to return already after I spoke for full rooms earlier in Zwolle and Veghel. We also continued there for a long time, with long chatting with each other. This is an important debate for the future of dairy farming. We have seen this in the great commitment. While two years ago it was thought that the end of milk quotas would offer opportunities in particular, concerns dominated mostly today. This debate brings greater clarity to that point, but for real certainty, at least another year has to be waited. This genuine certainty comes from Brussels, in the form of a new derogation, an exception to the Brussels fertilising rules. With this debate we are taking an important step forward in the discussions there. As I have noticed recently, many farmers are longing for the clarity that this debate can bring. It is therefore nice to be standing here and to be able to talk to the Chamber about two legislative proposals which have a major impact on the management of dairy farming. First of all, I will deal with the reason for the laws and the process towards them. Next, I will discuss the Law on soil-based growth dairy farming, with the corresponding amendments, then the amendment of the Fertilisers Act to introduce a system of phosphate rights, with the corresponding amendments, and finally I will address the questions that have been asked about the package to reduce phosphate production in 2017. The reason for this debate lies in the expiry of the milk quota. That offered great opportunities, was the expectation, because there was finally room to grow. Many farmers and sector organisations, and I believe some in the Chamber, celebrated this as a day of liberation, because it was finally possible to meet the rising demand on the world market. At that time, it seemed as if there was also scope for achieving this growth within the environmental boundary conditions. That offered a lot of choices, including for the farmers. Did they have to expand or not? There was also a warning at that time, here in the Chamber, as we noted last week, that it would cause problems if there were no new restrictions in place. However, scientific studies showed a different picture at the time. These studies provided for growth, in particular of milk production and the dairy herd, but the expectation of the LEI and the PBL was that it could all be within the environmental boundary conditions. The Chamber received those conclusions in the letter from my predecessor, Secretary of State Dijksma, dated December 2013. Mr Grashoff and some others asked. On the basis of the data available at the time, there was no reason to decide on production limiting measures, as stated in the December 2013 letter. That letter was widely supported in the Chamber. Indeed, there was a mandatory manure treatment system before and the December 2013 letter announced a system of responsible growth. In addition, the various sectors concerned themselves said that they would take measures to ensure that the growth of dairy farming would remain within the environmental limits. It has always been clear, however, that it has always been stated by my predecessor that, if the phosphate ceiling were to be exceeded, there would be irrevocable production-limiting measures. Since then, since December 2013, we have seen a lot of growth over the last three years. Farmers have chosen to do so, of course supported by financiers. We have tried to seize the opportunities that lie ahead as quickly as possible. This is reflected in the number of newly built stables and in the substantial increase in the number of cows. The result now, three years later, is that unfortunately this has created a great risk for Dutch dairy farming. Indeed, many dairy farmers make use of the derogation from the Nitrates Directive, the exception to fertilising rules. They are allowed to use more manure on their own land and thus reduce the costs for the disposal or treatment of manure that they would not otherwise have been able to use on their own land. However, the reason that the Netherlands has had that derogation since 2006 is therefore already ten years old... is not that financial advantage for dairy farmers. Dairy farming has become financially dependent on it over the years, but that is not why we get the EU's derogation. We get it because there is a solid scientific basis that in the Dutch climate grass and manure from grazing animals are a very good combination. The use of extra manure does not pose an increased risk to the quality of soil and surface water. This also confirms monitoring results. Groundwater quality among dairy farms using derogation is in principle better than groundwater quality among many other farms. It is therefore only right that the Netherlands should have a derogation. It is also desirable from an environmental point of view that the derogation should be retained. Without a derogation, less manure may be used on land, there will be a higher excess of manure, more fertilizer must be used, less grass will be left in our landscape and there will probably be fewer cows in the pasture. So there is not only an economic interest, but also a very great social interest; I would like to emphasise that. It is therefore very important that the Netherlands not only retain the current derogation, but can also make use of a new derogation from 2018. The Chamber can therefore rely on a number of Members of Parliament to ask that I and with me the entire team working on this at the Ministry and at the Dutch Representation in Brussels do everything in our power to obtain a new derogation. That trajectory starts this month. The Chamber will have the sixth action programme this month. That is the beginning of the path to a new derogation, starting from 2018. A number of Members of Parliament have asked for my commitment to what I said earlier about lowering the ceiling and possibly balancing it. The commitment described in the letter that the Chamber received from me on March 3rd of this year is like a house. That is what we are going to do: to negotiate a derogation which takes into account all the things contained in that letter. I will make every effort to do so. I will fight for that, I say to Mrs Dik-Faber, because she heard me say the same in Zwolle. I am fully convinced that it is important and good for the Netherlands. However, I would like to add that it is going to be quite a bone. I would like to make it clear that it is not natural and absolutely certain that the Netherlands can continue to make use of the derogation. The reason for this is well known to all of you. The derogation is also subject to a very specific condition: the amount of phosphate that can be produced in the Netherlands. That condition has been in place for many years and is therefore also known. In 2015, due to the rapid growth, we produced too much manure and thus too much phosphate. The phosphate ceiling will also be exceeded in 2016. Of course, we are very sorry about that, because in Brussels there is an agreement. We must also realise that derogation is not a right, but a favour given to us by the European Commission. The European Commission is advised on this by the Nitrate Committee, in which the Member States are represented. Today we are talking about what we have to do to restore the excess of the phosphate ceiling. A number of Members of Parliament have asked how this process has been conducted in recent times. On 2 July 2015, the Chamber received a letter from my predecessor, Mrs Dijksma, announcing that we would introduce a phosphate rights system in order to ensure that phosphate production would not exceed the ceiling. This has been the subject of direct discussions, including with representatives of the European Commission. In July 2015, consultations were held on this subject at various times. A lot of written information has also been exchanged. And we took the initiative to present the situation and the proposed approach in the Nitrates Committee last June, with which we also formally informed the other Member States. These contacts were, as usual, at all different levels: both at the technical level, at the high level of administration and at the political level. Coherence between these levels is essential. Some MPs noted that it was clear that there was State aid. They asked, "How could you not see that?" Let me make it quite clear: we have certainly seen this and certainly foreseen it. We assumed that the European Commission would come to the conclusion that there would be State aid. After all, it is a system of negotiable licences, rights are granted free of charge, they are freely negotiable, they will be scarce, and therefore they will be valued in economic terms. Mr Grashoff said: that makes sense. It is: it is logical that this is state aid. But that is not the issue at all. The question is: can state aid be compatible? Is it permissible on the basis of the European guidelines? To ensure that, you have guidelines that clarify the circumstances under which state aid may be permitted and under which circumstances not. Therefore, the legislation has been drawn up in such a way as to comply with the guidelines for environmental objectives and energy. That is why, immediately after the adoption of the legislative proposal by the Council of Ministers on 2 June this year, the consultation in Brussels was widened, including with the Commission's people involved in state aid, in accordance with the normal procedure in this type of procedure. I shall now go into the substance of the matter. It was only after the summer, mid-September, that it became clear that the European Commission did not want to see the phosphate rights system as lawful state aid. Of course, this was discussed very intensively with the European Commission at all levels, including at my level. Unfortunately, the conclusion of all these contacts was that the European Commission remained in line with its opinion. I will go into the substance of that judgment and also explain to the House why that was very unexpected for us. As I said, State aid can be declared compatible with the internal market by the European Commission. It was our commitment to ensure that state aid would also be declared as such. And it's not a matter of arbitrariness. It is not the case that an official in the European Commission wakes up badly one day and says: I do not declare it lawful this time. No, it's all fixed in guidelines. Those guidelines are pretty clear. Mr Dijkgraaf and Mrs Lodder also referred to this in the further report issued by the Chamber on the proposal for the phosphate rights system. Those guidelines state that State aid should not be granted for the purpose of compliance with Union standards. However, it explicitly states that a Union standard is not applicable where that standard applies to the level of the Member State. A Union standard exists when an individual company has to comply with a European rule. That is exactly the background to these state aid rules. They say, in fact, that if the EU makes rules for all companies, then of course a Member State cannot say: you know what I'm going to do, I'm going to favour my companies, I'm going to grant them subsidies to comply with those rules. That is what the State aid frameworks are for. What they emphasise is that standards adopted at EU level that are binding only on Member States are not considered to be Union standards. The European Commission was very surprised to see that it saw the national production ceiling from the derogation as a Union standard, a standard which would therefore apply to each individual undertaking. I hope that the Chamber can imagine that we were very uncomfortablely surprised by that judgment. We also looked at whether it was possible to challenge this judgment, so that phosphate rights can be introduced before 1 January 2017. The problem with this is that when you grant state aid, and that is the case when you grant marketable rights, which can only be done if that measure has been approved by the European Commission. If you provide that support when it has not been approved, it is by definition not allowed. You can have a dispute with the Commission, but you can't say: I'm doing it anyway. At that moment, the discussion will be over immediately. Then it is illegal state aid. And then what you have to do is recover that state aid from the recipients. And in this case, these are the dairy farmers. Those we want to help with this system would therefore be the ones to whom we would have to recover the value created by state aid. I have decided that I cannot, will not and therefore will not run, because it is a risk that I will walk with the dairy farmers' wallets. A corridor to the European Court of Justice was not an option either. In this situation too, the aid can only be granted once a formal authorisation has been received. So that would not have been helpful either. In October, therefore, I had no choice but to decide on the basis of the Commission's opinion, which I have already said was impossible to move. I had no choice but to amend the bill and allow the system as a whole to enter into force in 2018. The original bill provided for a first step in 2017, namely reducing production to the 2015 level. But it was not until 2018 that production had to be reduced to below the level of the derogation. The problem was caused by the explanation given by the European Commission to the State aid guidelines. Because the Commission says that there is an EU standard, which I very much dispute, the Commission also says that you should not apply state aid to comply with that standard. This also means that that opinion forces us to bring phosphate production under the ceiling by another route in 2017. The law wouldn't have done that anyway. If we had to go below the ceiling with phosphate production, private measures would have to be taken anyway. But through this judgment we have had to make a package that will lead to a sufficient reduction in phosphate production in 2017. I will come back to that package for 2017. I have a few more general questions and I would then like to discuss the legislation with the Chamber. Mrs Dik-Faber asked whether the Nitrate Directive was being exceeded. And is that only agriculture? At the end of June, the European Commission received the Nitrates Directive reporting for the previous period. The overall development is positive, but especially on the sandy soils in the south and east of the country nitrate values in groundwater are still higher than the standard value of 50 mg per litre. In part of the surface waters, also elsewhere than in the sandy area, there is eutrophication, which can partly be traced back to agriculture. The Nitrates Directive is also about reducing it. The new sixth action programme will therefore also include measures to prevent these exceedances. That is what we are going to discuss with the European Commission. Mr Geurts asked for the sixth action programme and for the whole of the next year to discuss the action programme and the derogation. He asked: can you inform us in advance? I'll do that. I will inform the Chamber for Christmas about my commitment to the Sixth Action Programme. Part of the justification required for the Sixth Action Programme is already available. The Court received the Nitrates Directive report in July and the derogation report. Another part of the evaluation of the Fertilisers Act is still working on the identification and justification of possible measures. I'll give it to the Chamber as soon as these are completed. Both Mr Geurts and Mrs Lodder asked: can we be kept informed of progress next year? Ms Lodders also asked to be kept informed of the progress of the 2017 package by receiving a quarterly report on it. I am prepared to inform the Chamber every quarter of the progress of discussions with Brussels. This applies both to the package and to the discussions on the Sixth Action Programme and the derogation. Mrs Lodders asked if I am prepared to support research into the recovery of phosphate from the innovation budget. There are funds in my budget for innovation in the top sector of Agri & Food. There is now a research programme on the added value of manure, of which the recovery of phosphate is an important aspect. Mr Graus asked about the implementation of his motion on the extraction of scarce phosphate, which is a valuable asset. My predecessor, Secretary of State Dijksma, informed the Chamber on 24 February 2015 in a letter explaining how the circular economy is being stimulated and how we can work on the use of renewable fertilisers. So that information has already been received by the Chamber.
ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u363	I am very surprised by the reasoning behind the state aid. It seems to be a very technical discussion whether or not something is a Union standard. If I try to translate it back into Dutch, it comes down to this. If you had to comply with the normal, strict EU standard, you should not help companies to comply, because this is state aid. But if you have been granted an exception for a much wider standard, it would therefore be no problem in the view of the Dutch government to grant state aid to meet that standard. ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u364	I should like to deal with Mr Grashoff's question in the debate on the law. He says, "You could have just chosen non-negotiable." I have, of course, looked at this very extensively. The consequence of non-tradeability is that everyone will do everything in their power to avoid it. Because you can always sell companies to someone else. If you want to transfer the rights that rest on a company to someone else, you sell the whole company. That way, you'll still be handing over rights. "Not negotiable" is theoretically possible, but in practice a way around it will be found. The second point is that you have to set up a system with regard to rights that may come back from people who say, "I quit my business. I'm not selling it, but I'm sending the rights back to the government." That will not be many, I think, but then the government will have to decide which company may or may not grow. This means that the government will then be the one to regulate the entire sector. Then you get a centrally managed dairy sector in the Netherlands. This deprives companies of the opportunity to make their own choices in the development of their business. It means that you are totally dependent on the regulations the government makes, where the government says: this company is allowed to grow and that company is not. If you fall out there, your company will be locked forever. I think that is a particularly undesirable situation. Dairy farmers are just free entrepreneurs and they need to be able to make their own choices about the development of their businesses. That can only be done when you make phosphate rights negotiable. That is not a bad system either, because we have it in the case of pig rights and in the case of poultry rights. Then we come to the question about the state aid aspects. These two systems have both been approved by the European Commission. The European Commission has not so much a problem with the system as with its introduction. The discussion is about the EU standard.

That is the system of compulsory manure treatment. The extraction of phosphate can also be considered. ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u395	Please continue.
ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u396	I come to the bill on ground-based growth. This bill implements the Reuten and Others motion, which was adopted unanimously in the Upper House at the end of 2014. It asked for the rules of the AMvB to be laid down in the law. That's what this bill does. In addition, I take the opportunity to remove some undesirable effects through two notes of amendment. That's exactly what I need to say. On the first letter of amendment, I would like to point out that we will explain a situation in which a dairy farm temporarily had an extraordinary amount of land in 2014, i.e. phosphate space, through participation in public nature development projects, in such a way that it may also be a situation in which a dairy farmer temporarily had extra land because on his land work was carried out for nature development on nearby land for which he had temporary replacement land in use. It is important to give that explanation of the bill. That makes it part of the legal history. A number of amendments have been tabled to this law. I believe that this will automatically bring me to the questions that have been asked. I shall begin with the amendment to item No. 14, by Mr Geurts and Mr Dik-Faber on feed-fertiliser contracts. This amendment provides that feed-fertiliser agreements may be included in the determination of the dairy phosphate surplus on the holding. This would allow more dairy farms to comply with land-based rules without acquiring land themselves. The underlying idea is to close the regional cycle. I am very positive about that idea, but I do think it is very important that the amendment also allows this room to examine whether the amendment does not substantially undermine the impact of the law on land-based growth in dairy farming and whether it is feasible. I'm worried about that. The amendment gives the scope to do the research and to include the impact on the legislative proposal on ground-based growth. Having said that, I can leave the opinion on this amendment to the Chamber.
ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u397	Do I understand correctly from the Secretary of State that he is going to investigate first whether this does not open a back door to grow without having ground?
ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u398	I'd rather say it in my own words, so it's clear. The amendment states that rules are laid down for General Measure of Governance. The explanatory statement makes it clear that there is a need to examine feasibility. That seems essential to me, because I am concerned that it is really feasible. In addition, consideration should be given to the impact of the deduction of feed-fertilisation agreements on the dairy phosphate surplus and to the consequences for the system of responsible and land-based growth dairy farming. The essence of the system of land-based growth is, of course, that you have to be able to justify your growth with land. The amendment aims to allow land to be counted and therefore to be regarded as 'accountable with land' if you have permanent contracts with someone in your immediate vicinity and therefore there is actually some sort of symbiosis between a farmer and a dairy farmer. We need to look very closely at the consequences of this. Of course, it is not intended to undermine the system of land-based growth and therefore to grow everywhere without sufficient land.

I have also indicated what the criteria are for this. I have instructed RVO.nl to carry out the system of fines permanently critically and to consider the special circumstances in which infringements are committed even more carefully when determining the highest of the fine. The Audit Service Rijk assesses the implementation practice of RVO.nl on these aspects. I will inform the Chamber of the results of this. If that is the case, I will, on the basis of that outcome, adjust the implementation of the penalty policy. Capturing circumstances that justify a lower fine in an AMvB makes future customization incredibly difficult. That is why I reject this amendment. I would like to thank the rapporteur for his excellent report. 17 the limits that are in the law, which were also in the AMvB, on how much percent of the extra-produced manure you have to process and how much you have to place on the ground, are actually adjusted.
