ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u426	Please continue.
ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u427	Mr. Chairman. We have already had a major debate on the Phosphate Act, in which many views have been exchanged and many questions have been answered. With your permission, I shall confine myself to answering the questions that have been asked on this subject and assessing the amendments that have been tabled. Mr Dijkgraaf asked about the sector ceiling and said that this is not the right starting point for this law, because other sectors also exceed. The derogation ceiling is about total phosphate production, he is right. We're gonna get us under there. One of the ways in which we do this is through the sector ceiling. We use the phosphate rights system to ensure that we do not exceed the total national ceiling. Several groups have asked questions about the skimming rate, the rate of reduction in transfer and the criteria of the phosphate bank. The generic rate of skimming shall be calculated on the basis, inter alia, of livestock size on 2 July 2015. I stress that 2 July 2015 is the target date, remains and will always be. The extent of the provision for cases of trouble, the relief of land-based businesses and the amendments adopted by the Chamber during this procedure also affect the generic rate of skimming, so I cannot calculate that at this time. The rate of skimming on transfer is 10. Mr Grashoff asked why it was 10%. It is intended to fill the phosphate bank, but it has been established that, on the one hand, there is still sufficient opportunity to promote desired development, so it must yield enough. On the other hand, it should not be an excessive obstacle to the development of individual companies. The skimming in transfer is also there to prevent speculation. A percentage of 10 also has a size with which you can do that. The criteria for the phosphate bank come with General Measure of Management. There are also amendments on this subject, but I will come to that later. This offers some flexibility to fill in the criteria. The Chamber shall be informed thereof. However, it is essential that these criteria go beyond EU standards for compatibility with the State aid frameworks. Mr Dijkgraaf, Mr Grashoff and Mr Van Gerven asked whether they could not do without marketable rights. I have just told Mr Grashoff why I find that undesirable. We have also been asked if we can arrange it for 2017 without being negotiable. That's not legally an option. The scheme would therefore be very vulnerable. On a large scale, promising claims can be expected, as from 2 July 2015 entrepreneurs have been announced that a system of marketable rights would be introduced, so that is what they have been able to take into account. If you deviate from that at this time, a month before the introduction, that is impossible for entrepreneurs to foresee, so that is not legally sustainable. That could be challenged in court, and most likely successfully. Mr Dijkgraaf and Mrs Dik-Faber have asked whether this generic skimming is sustainable because no one EP test has yet been carried out; this is a review of the European Convention on Human Rights, Article 1, which deals with property rights. This 1 EP test must be carried out when drawing up the General Measure of Governance. Of course, I will ensure that this General Measure is in line with the requirements of the European Convention on Human Rights. Then I perform the 1 EP-key, but I also focus the AMvB on that of course. Mr Dijkgraaf has asked whether it would not be good to convert pig rights to phosphate rights, and suggestions have also been made by others. The bill contains a provision allowing for the exchange of rights. There was also an amendment tabled by Members of this Chamber. I have always said: if it is considered desirable by the sectors, this provision can be used as far as I am concerned and then these rights could be transposed, but so far the sectors do not consider it desirable. Mrs Ouwehand had the impression that the Council of State and the state attorney cut the time limit. That's incorrect. On the contrary, both have said that the target date of 2 July 2015 is sustainable. Mrs Lodder asked about the assessment made by Security and Justice at her request. This clearly shows that the reference date is in any case legally sustainable. Mr Van Gerven asked a question about intensive farms that bought cows after the abolition of the milk quota. They come out well, if I may sum it up, because they are allowed to keep more animals than extensive farms that have not bought cows. This was one of the major issues for me when drafting this bill and when making the choices for my letter received by the Chamber on 3 March. We have also discussed this in the main debate. What I find most difficult to digest is that, legally speaking, we have no position to distinguish between growthers and non-growthers. I myself would have liked us to be able to target the growers. This was not legally sustainable because all this growth was legal. Entrepreneurs were therefore allowed to assume that their business could grow. After On 2 July 2015, Secretary of State Dijksma sent the letter to this Chamber and said that there would be a system of marketable phosphate rights. However, all growth that is still taking place after 2 July 2015 is at the expense and risk of the individual dairy farmer. All growth that took place after this date is therefore not translated into rights. Unfortunately, it was not possible to distinguish between companies which have and have not grown before that date in terms of growth. It is harsh, as I say here too, because it means that non-growers pay part of the bill of the total excess of the phosphate production ceiling, even though they have in fact contributed little to it. Legally, however, this is the only way to spread the pain among all companies. Mrs Lodder asked how to clarify the problems as quickly as possible and what route to take. The phosphate duties shall be introduced on the basis of the data for 2015. The most important data have already been submitted by the RVO to companies. In fact, therefore, they already have clarity about the data on the basis of which the allocation of phosphate rights is to be determined. I will make every effort to inform companies as well as possible in the run-up to 2018. I will also make it clear where companies that want to claim the bottleneck provision included in the legislative proposal can go. Mrs Ouwehand said that the bottleneck is not enough. Mrs Lodders asked about its sustainability and, in my opinion, about it. She did so in response to the advice of the Ministry of Security and Justice. A limited provision has been deliberately chosen as each bottleneck has to be met by increasing the total amount of phosphate duty being distributed and the generic skimming has to be increased to keep production below the ceiling. That is why there is a small problem regulation. This was tested again by the Ministry of Security and Justice. The Court also received that opinion. The Ministry has confirmed that, in view of the current circumstances, the emergency provision is satisfactory and legally sustainable. Mr Van Gerven gave some examples of biofarmers and asked what I could do for these people. I shall have the cases put forward by Mr Van Gerven examined. I'll give you a profit warning. An amendment has been tabled on this subject, but the current legislative proposal simply shuts down the bottleneck provision. If you are not covered, then you are not covered and then there are no possibilities to benefit from more phosphate rights. Mr Dijkgraaf asked another question about the spreading of young cattle. He said that this should actually be attributed to phosphate production. I think that's what Mrs Dik-Faber was asking for. Entrepreneurs can arrange that among themselves. The entrepreneur who gets rights for damaged animals can transfer those rights to the owner of the animals. Often the animals spend six months with the other. This can be agreed between us. The bill doesn't stand in the way of that. Mrs Ouwehand pointed out that the legislative proposal was still not actually adopted on the basis of the State aid frameworks, because the legislative proposal had to go beyond the EU standard, which the committee considered to be. The legislative proposal goes beyond the ceiling in the derogating decision. The allocation of rights takes place through the phosphate bank. The letter of amendment specifies that the allocation of rights through the phosphate bank should go beyond European environmental requirements. We are therefore achieving more ambitious objectives along this path than the objectives that the EU is demanding of us. I do not see the problems that Mrs Ouwehand envisages in this respect. Mrs Koşer Kaya asked whether a company could be converted into a legal person, so that one could avoid skimming when transferring. That's possible, but there's no way of knowing whether that's happening. Converting an ordinary business, often a one-man business or a vof, into another legal entity, for example, could be based on all kinds of other considerations, including financial considerations, which could weigh more heavily. So I can't say if companies are pre-sorting on that. They may have many other reasons to opt for a certain legal form. Mr Van Gerven asked about the predicted total value of all phosphate rights. It's 7 or 8 billion. What the value actually becomes depends on the market. That will be a question of supply and demand. Undoubtedly, there will be relatively high demand in the beginning, but it is certain that the total value is a theoretical amount. The rights will only be given value for companies selling their rights. For all other companies, it is only the right to be able to produce and thus run a business. Mrs Dik-Faber asked how I would like to prevent farmers from waiting for years for a favourable price for phosphate rights. How, or when, farmers decide to transfer their rights to someone else is up to those farmers themselves. Those are individual decisions that I can't steer in any way. There is no doubt that farmers will keep an eye on price formation. Mrs Dik-Faber said that many other parties, such as intermediaries, are likely to benefit. One can work with intermediaries, but it doesn't have to. It is up to entrepreneurs themselves to make that decision. The skimming in transfer prevents us from speculating. For every transfer, 10% of the rights must be surrendered. It is therefore necessary to make a great deal of money on the trade in phosphate rights if we are to benefit from it. That way, a barrier has been created. Mr Van Gerven asked whether there were no barriers to future regulation. That is not the case. The arrival of phosphate rights is not necessarily a terminal. It's one of the rules that businesses have to deal with. I refer to the pig and poultry sectors, which have long had production rights. The regulatory framework has subsequently been strengthened in other areas. For this sector, too, the manure processing obligation was introduced after production rights had already been in place. The government can do that without being liable for damages. This concern was raised by Mr Van Gerven. He asked if I could explain this in a letter, but I hope that I gave him sufficient information with this answer. Mrs Ouwehand asked whether the definition of land-boundness is correct, since the use of land by an intensive farmer counts to a leliteler. She was concerned about the consequences for biofarmers. The land used in the holding on 15 May of the year in question shall be used to determine the soil density of a holding. This is a simple and effective way of determining soil density, not only in the context of manure, but also in the context of the common agricultural policy. It prevents a piece of land from being abandoned by two farmers in one year. I wouldn't trade that in for a more practical system. Mrs Ouwehand and others referred to organic farming. There are organic farmers who are not land-bound. There aren't many of them, but they're there. That is just what the rules apply to all non-ground farmers. So if you have a biological business and are not ground-bound, you produce a surplus of manure, just like usual farms, and that surplus has to be disposed of elsewhere. These farms also contribute to the phosphate production of dairy farming as a whole. They cannot therefore be excluded from the shrinkage either. For land-based farmers, by far the largest part, the provision in the legislative proposal is that they are kept as safe as possible. I didn't fill in the bill to what extent, because I thought that should be discussed in the Chamber. The current bill offers the opportunity to spare these farmers to the maximum of the latent space. There is an amendment tabled by the House of Commons which I shall come up with in a moment to go a step further. Mr Graus asked how it was possible that a biofarmer should not offer manure to individuals. Cattle farmers may dispose of up to 250 kilos of phosphate to private individuals. It is therefore not prohibited, but there is a limitation, a maximum quantity. Mr Van Gerven said that the intensification of dairy farming is detrimental to pig farming. He asked how many more pig farmers were going to pay for manure disposal. The sale price for manure depends on several factors, including the supply and growth of manure production. Manure from dairy farming can indeed have a price-raising effect on manure from pig farming. I cannot answer the question as to whether or to what extent this is the case, because I do not have any data on it. Mr Grashoff had previously asked for figures which I could not give him at the time. In the meantime, I've sent it to him. I think I sent him exactly the numbers he asked for. If that is not the case, it is annoying, but I have literally answered Mr Grashoff's question. I think those were the questions that were asked. Soon I will come to a number of questions relating to amendments. These will naturally be dealt with in the discussion of the relevant amendments.

Now groundboundness and pasture don't always have to be the same. Groundboundness is about placing manure, but does not always mean that the cows come out. This will ensure that everyone will choose what is most advantageous for him or her and thus means that more phosphate rights will be allocated and that the generic discount will have to be increased. For example, dairy cows will also be counted twice. This leads to a higher generic discount. I do not think that is desirable, and for that reason I would advise against this amendment. The amendment in part No. 31 concerns the total relief of land-based holdings. I have always said, both in the letter of 3 March and here in the main debate we had in April, that I want to spare as much ground-based companies as possible. Land-based farms contribute to total phosphate production, but in fact do not contribute to the total phosphate surplus, because they can dispose of all manure on their own land. This is why the legislative proposal includes an article that allows land-based farms to keep up with their dairy phosphate space. I have always said that I think I should discuss again with the Chamber the extent to which this is happening. A motion for a resolution was tabled in the main debate which called for the total relief of land-based companies. This has consequences for the non-land-based companies. On the basis of the figures we are now familiar with, there is a need for a general reduction of about 2.5% for non-ground-based companies. That's the consequence. I believe that the Chamber should make a proper assessment of this. There is also a sub-amendment tabled by Mr Van Gerven. I will come to that in detail later. This ensures that the boundary between ground-based and non-ground-based is not as tight as the result of this amendment alone. That would mean that you would be completely spared if you produced just 1 kilo less phosphate than the ground density standard, but that you would be completely reduced if you produced just 1 kilo more. Secondly, I come to the amendment on item No. 34 of Mr Geurts and Mr Dik-Faber on the generic reduction of AMvB for poultry and pig farming. In this bill, I have included a hook to skim poultry and pig rights, as well as phosphate rights. This amendment removes the possibility of a generic reduction of poultry and pig rights, should one or both of the sectors exceed the production ceiling, which was the case last year, for example. I would like to make it clear to Members that this is the consequence. If this is deleted, this means the following. If there is a national overrun of the phosphate production ceiling, there is only one sector that can be reduced, namely dairy farming, even if it is not responsible for the overrun. In other words, if the overrun occurs elsewhere, in pig farming or in poultry farming, the only option is to cut down on dairy farming. I think the Court should take this into account. I'll leave that judgment to the Chamber. However, this is the consequence of this amendment. This is also why I included the hook in the bill.

ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u479	That's perfectly clear. I come to the amendment to item No. 36 by Mr Geurts and Mrs Dik-Faber. It says: You may rent up to 30% of phosphate rights. I have also explained in the explanatory statement, and also in the explanatory statement, why I did not choose to do so. In my view, tax depreciation actually reduces the burden on the purchase of phosphate rights. I believe that this can have a price increase effect and that this will increase the price of phosphate rights. At the same time, you can say that tax depreciation can stimulate transfer. It is not certain what the effect of this is. The following, however, is perhaps more important to me and is why I did not choose it. This amendment really puts an end date in the law. If, therefore, phosphate rights cannot lapse by 2023, the law must be amended accordingly. It's not my preference to do it that way. We have also seen in the case of pig rights and poultry rights that the government or politics cannot always predict how long such rights are needed. Therefore, if the Court wants tax depreciation, it would be my preference to note that the rights by Royal Decree may lapse at a time to be determined. I prefer that to a fixed term in the law. Therefore, since there is a fixed deadline, I must advise against the amendment.

ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u488	Okay, now that's the Amendment No. 46. I hope it has not changed to such an extent that my argument is no longer valid, but I do not suspect that. The amendment does not actually provide for the amount of phosphate duty to be brought into line with the phosphate production ceiling. That actually undermines what this law is intended to do, so I must strongly discourage the amendment. I come to the amendment tabled by Mr Geurts and Mrs Dik-Faber to item No. 40, a veil for the ministerial arrangement. That's about the KringloopWijzer. Why are we introducing a system of phosphate rights instead of animal rights? Because the cabinet wants dairy farmers to be able to control phosphate production on their farms. This amendment is in line with that. I also want dairy farmers who deal efficiently with phosphate to be rewarded with more space to undertake. This amendment regulates this and asks the Cabinet to do everything in its power to report to the Chamber. I leave the opinion on this amendment to the Chamber. I would like to say once again that this is going in the direction I am aiming for. I come to the amendment to item No. 48, on the exemption system of the phosphate bank. This amendment allocates skimmed phosphate rights to farmers and makes them freely negotiable again. However, the bill states that the phosphate bank grants exemptions which are not negotiable. The phosphate bank ensures that a situation goes beyond just securing the phosphate production ceiling is being worked on. That objective was explicitly included in the legislative proposal with the letter of amendment in order to respond to the concerns expressed by the European Commission. An exemption shall be granted on an individual basis, subject to conditions to be determined. Precisely in those conditions to be defined is the fact that you go beyond European standards. By opting for the re-issue of phosphate rights that are transferable, the link to those conditions cannot be made and the legislative proposal no longer meets the European Commission's State aid criteria. I must therefore strongly discourage the amendment. In the amendment to item No. 49, by Mr Geurts and Mrs Dik-Faber, states that up to 10% of phosphate rights can be transferred without skimming them. The aim is to give the dairy farmer a little more flexibility to align phosphate production by leasing with the number of phosphate rights on his holding at the end of a calendar year. To this end, the transfer of 10% of phosphate duties is exempted from skimming. This gives rise to structures where rights are transferred in sections. Moreover, it is unknown whether transfer results from sale, rental or lease. Moreover, the skimming is 10% and 10% of 10% means a skimming of 1%. The skimming can therefore be monitored for dairy farmers who use it. It does lead to less filling of the phosphate bank. I reject this amendment because of all these arguments. I come to the amendment to item No. 50. Mrs Lodders actually says that the legislative proposal should only enter into force if there is a real view of a derogation. Let me be clear: this bill is intended to meet the phosphate production ceiling, which is a condition for derogation. If we were not to have a derogation and therefore no longer have a phosphate production ceiling, it would not be obvious to work with a system of phosphate rights. To this end, the amendment provides for a preliminary procedure. The entry into force of the legislative proposal is conditional on the likelihood that the European Commission will grant a new derogation. My commitment is to ensure timely clarity on derogation. I would point out to the Chamber that the last time the derogation was granted was only in April 2014, when the period started on 1 January 2014. It is therefore not certain that we already have clarity about derogation from the phosphate rights system. Mrs Lodder's aim, as I read in the explanatory statement, is that at that time the cabinet makes it clear that there is a view of a new derogation. Having said that, I can leave the opinion on the amendment to the Chamber.
ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u489	Again my call which I also made in my first term. This means that the Secretary of State must go to Brussels very quickly, so already. ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u490	We do everything on our side, I do everything on it, but it takes two to tango. It's not like I can force it when it's discussed. The following amendment states that there is a heavy risk in the conversion of production rights and stipulates that it must not lead to the emission of more greenhouse gases. The legislative proposal already provides for this condition, because transposition should not lead to an increase in nitrogen compounds. The amendment lays down a heavy procedure for the AMvB in which the transposition is further regulated. As I have already pointed out in other amendments, I think that is too much of a barrier to the measure we are talking about. I therefore advise against this amendment.
ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u491	I have just lost the number of the amendment, but it is an amendment of my hand.
ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u492	It is a question of the amendment to item No. 52.
ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u493	Do I understand correctly from the Secretary of State that it is already legally guaranteed that the amount of greenhouse gases cannot increase in the transfer of rights or is only the heavy veil the bottleneck?
ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u494	Especially the front is the bottleneck. It already contains a provision stating that the transposition must take into account a number of substances relevant to the environment. I should like to make a specific observation as to whether this also concerns greenhouse gases. I think that Mr Van Gerven is also concerned with methane emissions. I have focused on, for example, ammonia emissions. The other nitrogen compounds were at least included in the legislative proposal.
ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u495	I shall, of course, look at that heavy veil, but perhaps the Secretary of State can confirm that in the second term or later. Of course, we are concerned that it is not possible for us to have an environmental degradation if we do not adopt this amendment.
ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u496	I will come back to these greenhouse gases in the second term for a moment. The Dik-Faber amendment to item No. 53 is about a veil for the AMvB on the determination of the generic discount rate. I have in the amendment to item No. 6 already indicated that this does not fit in with the timetable needed to determine and implement that generic skimming in time. I therefore advise against the amendment. The amendment in part No. 55 is Mr Van de Gerven's sub-amendment. I have already said that I am in agreement with the amendment on item No. 31 can embrace. I shall therefore leave it to the Chamber to decide. In the Geurts c.s. amendment in piece No. 56 is actually the possibility of extending the problem provision for general administrative action on the basis of an opinion from a committee of wise men. The problem provision included in the legislative proposal only concerns companies that are disproportionately disadvantaged by the date of 2 July 2015. This has been deliberately chosen to limit the level of the necessary generic discount. By expanding the bottled supply, the size of the generic discount could increase significantly, especially if the bottled supply is also seen on latent stable space or on latent placement space on land. However, the amendment also states that the Committee of Wise Men should also be outside the scope of this amendment, including the consequences of possible enlargement. Having said that, I leave this amendment to the House. The Dijkgraaf amendment in part No. 57 also contains an entry into force clause, but this requires further conditions and goes a few steps further than the provision proposed by Mrs Lodders. I think Mrs Lodder's provision is workable, but not because it goes too far in terms of terms and dates. That is why I must strongly advise against this amendment. In the amended Van Gerven amendment to item No. 58 it is proposed that land-based companies should be granted phosphate rights in the event of land-based expansion. For this purpose, non-land-based farms need to be cut twice a year. You can't just do that. This requires a test of Article 1, First Protocol ECHR, as has already been said. Shortening non-ground dairy farmers twice a year cannot be justified on the basis of that test. That is why I must strongly advise against this amendment. In the amended Van Gerven amendment to item No. 59 proposes that mega-stables should not be granted new rights. There is no good argument as to why this legislative proposal should limit the absolute size of dairy farms. I do not see any reason to do so in this bill, and I therefore advise against this amendment. With his amendment to item No. 66 Mr Van Gerven wants to cut down common farms for the benefit of organic farms. The amendment regulates the right of ownership. This requires further sound justification in the light of Article 1, First Protocol ECHR. The additional generic discount is in addition to the already foreseen generic discount necessary to bring the number of phosphate rights into line with the production ceiling. In this case, there is no need for a reduction. I doubt to what extent this could pass a test on the basis of the article I have just mentioned. I therefore reject this amendment. In the amended Van Gerven amendment to item No. 61 it is proposed that land-based companies should be completely exempt from generic discounts in case of a transfer discount. I have said before that shorting on transfer is necessary to fill the phosphate bank. The phosphate bank is also needed to ensure that the legislative proposal complies with the European State aid frameworks. By avoiding soil-based farms from skimming, it becomes considerably more difficult to meet this requirement, as just under half of the farms are ground-bound. There will also be a very big difference between farms that are just land-based and those that are not. I therefore reject this amendment. In the amended Van Gerven amendment to item No. 64 it is proposed that phosphate rights are not negotiable but distributed via a ZBO. Not only did we discuss this extensively in April, but also just now. I strongly advise against this amendment. It's a completely different model than I'm proposing in the bill. In the Dijkgraaf amendment to part No. 65 it is proposed that a mixed holding may abolish pig or poultry duties and convert them into exemptions for phosphate rights. The current legislative proposal already provides for the possibility of converting production rights through an AMvB. The amendment therefore seems superfluous. However, as has already been said, the sectors do not yet see any transposition of one right into another. There is a desire to maintain the sector ceilings. I do not think that an amendment which offers a variation on this transposition, namely at company level, can be supported by the sector. That is why I do not recommend this amendment. The main reason is that it is superfluous compared to what is already there.
ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u497	Since the LTOs of this world do not agree, an individual farmer who has chickens, pigs and cows within his company may not do so, while it is not transferable to another company.
ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u498	When you are allowed to do this within your company, what will prevent you from buying up pig rights, for example, as a dairy farm, and then converting them into phosphate rights?

Pig rights are much cheaper per kilo of phosphate than phosphate rights. Then dairy farming will suck out pig farming if you actually open the lock with this amendment all at once.
ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u501	Go on!
ParlaMint-NL_2016-12-01-tweedekamer-13.u502	The amendment in part No. 66 concerns the establishment of detailed rules for the transfer of phosphate rights at AMvB. That transition will be possible as of 1 January 2018. The AMvB must therefore enter into force on that date. I have in the amendment to item No. 6 already indicated that it is not realistic in the timetable to do all that. I must therefore discourage the amendment. In the amendment to item No. 67 calls for an exemption for holdings with less than 2.5 cows per hectare. Thus, by exempting part of dairy farming from phosphate rights, you cannot send for that either. You basically undermined your steering device. The aim of this system is not to produce too much phosphate. It also ignores the agreement with the European Commission that the total phosphate production is relevant to the ceiling provided for in the derogation. I therefore reject the amendment. In her amendment to item No. Mrs Dik-Faber spoke about an exception for beef cuts. That amendment stipulates that there can be set-off between two farms in order to correct for the cutting-in and cutting-out of dairy cows. Such netting shall not be regarded as a transfer subject to skimming. In doing so, Mrs Dik-Faber has a possible solution to the issue of beef and veal. I'll leave that judgment to the Chamber. Finally, I promised Mrs Lodder that we would return to this block on the question of the blank phosphate reference. All farmers whose RVO assumes they are dealing with the phosphate rights system have had a so-called service letter. Most of the companies over 17,000 have received a letter containing data. Other companies were asked to provide data because the data were not fully known. All entrepreneurs can submit their views. These are processed for the ultimate granting of the rights. This process often also runs in consultation with the relevant entrepreneurs in order to achieve a common clarity. That must therefore be done in the near future by joint agreement. This brings me to the last block, about the 2017 package. As I said earlier, we need to take measures to comply not only with what the European Commission requires by 1 January 2018, namely to ensure that we no longer exceed the phosphate production ceiling, but also to introduce the phosphate rights system only if we are no longer in breach of the phosphate production ceiling. A package of measures should therefore be taken in 2017 to reduce phosphate production below the phosphate production ceiling. Based on the most up-to-date data, the Central Bureau of Statistics calculated that phosphate production would be exceeded by 6.6 million kilos in 2016. The assignment for 2017 is to produce at least 6.6 million kilos less than in 2016. I spoke to LTO Nederland, NZO, Nevedi, NajK, NMV and Rabobank on 17 November. Network Fundamental and Stichting Natuur en Milieu were present. With the parties I mentioned first, I was able to reach agreement on a package of measures for the reduction of phosphate production in 2017. This package is based on the initiative of the various sector parties. They have looked at the measures they can take in their own right. So the lead lies in the business community. In doing so, it takes responsibility for the problem that has arisen and for its solution. Where possible, I support implementation and legal and financial resources. I've informed the Chamber about that. There are three main measures: the feed track, the reduction of the dairy herd through shrinkage or stopper schemes and the phosphate reduction plan of ZuivelNL. At least 1.7 million kilograms will be reduced by means of the transport track. This consists of tightening the maximum standards for phosphorus content in compound feed in an existing agreement between Nevedi, LTO Netherlands, NZO and VLB. Mr Grashoff asked whether this was intended to allow livestock to grow. No, that's not what it's for. The point is that the feed for the current livestock contains less phosphorus and so the manure also contains less phosphate. By reducing the dairy herd, phosphate production is reduced by 2.5 million kilograms. It's about dairy farmers who want to end the farm. There are also opportunities to support shrinkage. The number of stoppers has decreased in recent years. The expectation is that people are waiting for clarity about the phosphate rights system and so there are many stoppers who might be encouraged to make their decision to stop. For this they get a premium per dairy cow which they reduce. EUR 50 million is available for this measure. A large proportion of these resources come from the national envelope under EU action. The scheme will be opened in at least three tranches and the premium will always be slightly reduced per tranche. ZuivelNL's phosphate reduction plan aims to achieve 4 million kilograms of phosphate reduction by reducing the number of cows. This task is divided among the dairy companies, which transfer it back to the dairy farmers. The starting point is the growth that dairy farms have experienced after the date of 2 July 2015. Dairy companies may opt for a milk payment scheme or a dairy reduction scheme. This depends on the legal form and management of the dairy company. The milk money scheme is a bonus-malus system, where the milk supplied too much is malus. The proceeds are used to pay a bonus on the number of animals that a dairy farmer has shrunk. In the dairy reduction scheme, the reduction in livestock units is directly passed on to the dairy farmer, with the excess LUs being disposed of within a certain period of time. These three measures add up to a reduction of 8.2 million kilograms. That is enough to achieve the 6.6 million kilograms and to cope with any setbacks. In addition, I am still in consultation on measures that can strengthen the package. These are additional feed measures for the dairy sector. There might still be opportunities. Measures may also be taken by pig farming. This could also contribute to further reduction of total phosphate production in the Netherlands. I am in consultation with the European Commission on the 2017 package of measures. Some people asked about it. Its purpose is twofold. We are discussing with the Commission's environmental services that this package is sufficiently convincing and effective. We are also discussing its monitoring to ensure that they are provided with the data that will allow them to see that actual progress is being made. We are also talking to the people of the agricultural services and the competition services about clarity about the applicable frameworks for state aid, competition and the internal market. Together with the sector parties that have drawn up the plans, I am committed to ensuring that the first parts of the package can start from 1 January 2017. I would like to say to Mrs Ouwehand, who was concerned about the level of support, that through these sector parties broad support has been secured. Mrs Dik-Faber asked how we are watching this. I will, of course, closely monitor what this package brings. We will also have to discuss this with the Commission, because we will have to show that it works. Mr Van Gerven asked for further information. I will inform the Chamber before the end of the year about the package and the process that will take place in 2017. Mr Geurts and others asked how the package fits the law we are discussing today. The 2017 package ensures that phosphate production is brought into line with the phosphate ceiling. This will allow for the effective introduction of the phosphate rights system on 1 January 2018. This should then ensure that phosphate production remains below the ceiling. I am prepared to discuss with the sector whether the outcome of this debate and therefore the votes on the amendments can be taken into account in the package. I assume that the votes will take place next Tuesday. I am prepared to look with the sector at how this can be translated into the measures taken by the sector itself. This will include in particular the phosphate reduction plan of ZuivelNL. Mr Leenders asked how hard this package of measures was. I think it's an ambitious package. It's going a long way. If we look at the figures we have now, the package goes a lot further than what is needed to bring phosphate production back below the ceiling in 2017. It also shows that everyone has committed themselves to that. There are, of course, major interests at stake for the dairy companies, the dairy farmers, the bank and the feed companies. So they have all committed themselves to this so that this can be done. Questions have been asked about monitoring progress. I instruct the CBS to monitor the development of phosphate production next year throughout the year. On the basis of these results, any adjustments may be made and the Commission may be informed. In reply to Mrs Lodder's questions, I have already said that I am prepared to inform the Chamber about progress every quarter. The SP asked why the land-based farmers weren't allowed to talk. Network Thorough sat at the table with the director group and was therefore also at the discussion of the proposals. They all sat at the table and they also represent land-based companies. After all, they are members of all these different organisations. Will land-based companies now also be spared? I have just said that I am prepared to discuss this with the sector, should this Chamber adopt the amendment in question. I know that ZuivelNL is following this debate with particular interest, and that is certainly true of this outcome. Mr Leender asked whether the responsibility now lies with the chain parties, because they have taken the lead in the action. The lead is indeed there. I support implementation and development wherever possible, but we are all in favour of achieving the result. It is also an interest that we share. Mr Dijkgraaf asked whether it is all legally sustainable and whether there are no implementation problems because not everyone agrees. The NZO, LTO Nederland, the NMV and ZuivelNL support this plan. They are going to ask for this measure to be made generally binding. As a result, the sector is bound in full width. I have promised to do everything I can to do that. At the moment, ZuivelNL is busy working on the plan. I support them in this, too, so that the measures are designed to ensure that they are legally sustainable. This is something that needs to be worked out now to ensure that sustainability is guaranteed. The sector and I do not see any need at the moment for a problem regulation. The sector is also working on a more detailed approach to the proposals. Mr President, I think I have answered almost all the questions. There were a couple of double questions. I advise against this motion. It's just a question of whether you produce more manure than you can lose on your land. Of the non-ground organic farms, there are only a few, but they are not ground-bound anyway.
